Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. And... well, if anyone even mentions the showcase, I usually just change the subject right away, or I make an excuse to leave before I have to talk about it. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. But I definitely try to avoid anything that might trigger them. \n\nUm, for example, I've been taking the long way around campus every day just so I don't have to walk past the atrium where it happened. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Ever since then, I've had this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I feel like I'm constantly on edge now. Um, for example, if a door bangs shut, or even if a cart just rattles down the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then... it takes me several minutes just to stop shaking and get back to what I was doing. It's like I just can't relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations since the lighting rig collapsed at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. And... well, if anyone even mentions the showcase, I usually just change the subject right away, or I make an excuse to leave before I have to talk about it. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. But I definitely try to avoid anything that might trigger them. \n\nUm, for example, I've been taking the long way around campus every day just so I don't have to walk past the atrium where it happened. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or having difficulty connecting with friends and family since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. I'm not avoiding them or anything... it's really just the campus stuff and the showcase that I'm staying away from.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty in connecting emotionally with your friends and family since the incident?\n\nPatient: I know I just said I'm not avoiding them, but... I guess emotionally, it has been different. Like, the other day, my husband cut some flowers from the garden and tried to make me tea. And I knew it was a really kind thing for him to do, but I just couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure I normally would from something like that. I just felt this... blank heaviness instead. So, yeah, I suppose connecting in that way has been difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in things. I still care about my hobbies and the things I usually like to do. I mean, it's a bit harder to feel the joy in them right now, like I mentioned with my husband, but the interest itself is still there. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't actually there. It's really just that disconnected feeling I mentioned... but I always know what's real and what isn't. It just feels strange to look at it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this disconnected feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been happening for about six days now. It started right after... um, there was an incident at work during a showcase. A lighting rig collapsed. Ever since then, I've had this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I feel like I'm constantly on edge now. Um, for example, if a door bangs shut, or even if a cart just rattles down the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then... it takes me several minutes just to stop shaking and get back to what I was doing. It's like I just can't relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations since the lighting rig collapsed at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. And... well, if anyone even mentions the showcase, I usually just change the subject right away, or I make an excuse to leave before I have to talk about it. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. But I definitely try to avoid anything that might trigger them. \n\nUm, for example, I've been taking the long way around campus every day just so I don't have to walk past the atrium where it happened. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or having difficulty connecting with friends and family since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. I'm not avoiding them or anything... it's really just the campus stuff and the showcase that I'm staying away from.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty in connecting emotionally with your friends and family since the incident?\n\nPatient: I know I just said I'm not avoiding them, but... I guess emotionally, it has been different. Like, the other day, my husband cut some flowers from the garden and tried to make me tea. And I knew it was a really kind thing for him to do, but I just couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure I normally would from something like that. I just felt this... blank heaviness instead. So, yeah, I suppose connecting in that way has been difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in things. I still care about my hobbies and the things I usually like to do. I mean, it's a bit harder to feel the joy in them right now, like I mentioned with my husband, but the interest itself is still there. I still want to do those things.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you feel like you're watching everything through that piece of glass, like you described earlier?\n\nPatient: It mostly happens when I walk across campus. That's honestly what scares me the most about all of this. Whenever I'm there, it just feels like... like I'm watching everything through that glass, like I'm not fully in my own body. The buildings, the students walking by... everything around me just seems slightly unreal. So, I guess it's pretty much every time I have to be on campus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you. Well, I'm here because I've been having these strange experiences lately that are really starting to bother me. I work as a teaching assistant, and what scares me most is that when I walk across campus, it feels like I’m watching everything through a piece of glass. It's... it's like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just seem slightly unreal. I'm just hoping to figure out what's going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced hearing or seeing things that aren't there when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't actually there. It's really just that disconnected feeling I mentioned... but I always know what's real and what isn't. It just feels strange to look at it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this disconnected feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been happening for about six days now. It started right after... um, there was an incident at work during a showcase. A lighting rig collapsed. Ever since then, I've had this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I feel like I'm constantly on edge now. Um, for example, if a door bangs shut, or even if a cart just rattles down the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then... it takes me several minutes just to stop shaking and get back to what I was doing. It's like I just can't relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations since the lighting rig collapsed at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. And... well, if anyone even mentions the showcase, I usually just change the subject right away, or I make an excuse to leave before I have to talk about it. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. But I definitely try to avoid anything that might trigger them. \n\nUm, for example, I've been taking the long way around campus every day just so I don't have to walk past the atrium where it happened. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or having difficulty connecting with friends and family since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. I guess emotionally, it has been different. Like, the other day, my husband cut some flowers from the garden and tried to make me tea. And I knew it was a really kind thing for him to do, but I just couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure I normally would from something like that. I just felt this... blank heaviness instead. So, yeah, I suppose connecting in that way has been difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in things. It's... it's like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just seem slightly unreal. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't actually there. It's really just that disconnected feeling I mentioned... but I always know what's real and what isn't. It started right after... um, there was an incident at work during a showcase. A lighting rig collapsed. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or having difficulty connecting with friends and family since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you. Well, I'm here because I've been having these strange experiences lately that are really starting to bother me. I work as a teaching assistant, and what scares me most is that when I walk across campus, it feels like I’m watching everything through a piece of glass. It's... it's like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just seem slightly unreal. I'm just hoping to figure out what's going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced hearing or seeing things that aren't there when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't actually there. It's really just that disconnected feeling I mentioned... but I always know what's real and what isn't. It just feels strange to look at it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this disconnected feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been happening for about six days now. It started right after... um, there was an incident at work during a showcase. A lighting rig collapsed. Ever since then, I've had this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I feel like I'm constantly on edge now. Um, for example, if a door bangs shut, or even if a cart just rattles down the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then... it takes me several minutes just to stop shaking and get back to what I was doing. It's like I just can't relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations since the lighting rig collapsed at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. I guess emotionally, it has been different. Like, the other day, my husband cut some flowers from the garden and tried to make me tea. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you. Well, I'm here because I've been having these strange experiences lately that are really starting to bother me. I work as a teaching assistant, and what scares me most is that when I walk across campus, it feels like I’m watching everything through a piece of glass. It's... it's like I’m not fully in my own body, and the buildings and the students around me just seem slightly unreal. I'm just hoping to figure out what's going on with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced hearing or seeing things that aren't there when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had anything like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't actually there. It's really just that disconnected feeling I mentioned... but I always know what's real and what isn't. It just feels strange to look at it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this disconnected feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been happening for about six days now. It started right after... um, there was an incident at work during a showcase. A lighting rig collapsed. Ever since then, I've had this feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really have. I feel like I'm constantly on edge now. Um, for example, if a door bangs shut, or even if a cart just rattles down the hallway, I jump so hard my chest actually hurts. And then... it takes me several minutes just to stop shaking and get back to what I was doing. It's like I just can't relax at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, activities, or situations since the lighting rig collapsed at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Um, I've actually been taking the long way around campus just so I don't have to pass by the atrium. And... well, if anyone even mentions the showcase, I usually just change the subject right away, or I make an excuse to leave before I have to talk about it. I just really don't want to be near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things, like sights, sounds, or smells, that remind you of the lighting rig collapse and cause you to feel anxious or avoid them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's mostly sounds. The actual sound of that rig coming down just... it keeps barging into my head out of nowhere. I'll just be sitting at my desk grading papers, or even just making coffee, and it hits me. And suddenly I can see the volunteer on the floor, and all the students pushing forward... it just feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nDoctor: Has this feeling of being disconnected or your anxiety been affecting your ability to do your job as a teaching assistant or your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely has. I haven't been able to do my job normally at all. Um, the other day I actually had to just get up and walk out of a planning meeting. I've also missed two mentoring sessions with my students this week. And like I said before, I'm avoiding parts of campus that I really need to use for my job. It's just getting really hard to function.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to push away or avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when it comes to mind?\n\nPatient: I'm not exactly sure. I mean, I guess I try not to dwell on it, but... like I said, the memories just kind of happen whether I want them to or not. I don't really know if I'm actively pushing the thoughts away. I mostly just try to force myself to get back to grading or whatever I was doing when it hits me.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the lighting rig collapse when memories of it come to your mind?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a moment ago, the thoughts themselves kind of just force their way in whether I want them to or not. But I definitely try to avoid anything that might trigger them. \n\nUm, for example, I've been taking the long way around campus every day just so I don't have to walk past the atrium where it happened. And if anyone brings up the showcase, I just immediately change the subject... or I'll literally just leave the room before I have to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or having difficulty connecting with friends and family since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't noticed any issues with that. I mean, I still talk to my family and my friends pretty much like normal. I'm not avoiding them or anything... it's really just the campus stuff and the showcase that I'm staying away from.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty in connecting emotionally with your friends and family since the incident?\n\nPatient: I know I just said I'm not avoiding them, but... I guess emotionally, it has been different. Like, the other day, my husband cut some flowers from the garden and tried to make me tea. And I knew it was a really kind thing for him to do, but I just couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure I normally would from something like that. I just felt this... blank heaviness instead. So, yeah, I suppose connecting in that way has been difficult.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the incident at work?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in things. I still care about my hobbies and the things I usually like to do. I mean, it's a bit harder to feel the joy in them right now, like I mentioned with my husband, but the interest itself is still there. I still want to do those things.\n\nDoctor: How often would you say you feel like you're watching everything through that piece of glass, like you described earlier?\n\nPatient: It mostly happens when I walk across campus. That's honestly what scares me the most about all of this. Whenever I'm there, it just feels like... like I'm watching everything through that glass, like I'm not fully in my own body. The buildings, the students walking by... everything around me just seems slightly unreal. I just can't bring myself to go near it or discuss it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your avoidance of the atrium and discussions about the showcase is interfering with your daily life or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely is.
